# Review tracker

What Alex has checked, and what still needs checking. One row per published page (redirect pages left out).

**Status:** ✅ Checked (by Alex, with date) · 🔍 To review (something new or changed since Alex last looked) · ❔ Not recorded (built before this tracker; Alex may already have checked it).

**Rule for every chat:** when you build or change a page, set its row to 🔍 To review and say what to look at. Only Alex marks a page ✅. When he says a page is checked, set ✅ with the date and clear its "What to check".

**Draft tags (11 Nov 2026):** every page whose row here is not ✅ shows "Draft" on the page and on its list cards (`tools/review/drafts.py sync`, run on every push). Alex marks a page checked from the page itself: open any page once with `?reviewer`, then press "✓ Checked" and submit the GitHub issue it opens; the row turns ✅ "✓ button" and the tag goes. A new page needs a row here, or it gets no Draft tag.

| Calculation Generator `calculation-generator.html` | 🔍 To review | 12 Nov 2026 (s.f., Alex): Significant figures now works like the exam. The values are given to 2 or 3 s.f. (never 1, never ambiguous like 1450 or 40), the question says "Give your answer to an appropriate number of significant figures", and the answer goes to the fewest s.f. in the data, never fewer than 2. A fixed value such as 230 V or 4200 J/kg °C counts too; where it could be read either way, both answers are accepted. Take g and c are not counted, and the nudged values keep the story (a tyre that warms up still ends hotter). A right value with the wrong s.f. gets its own message. 12 Nov 2026 (later still, Alex): Tick all button back, beside Clear ticks; every equation is ticked each time the page opens. 12 Nov 2026 (later, Alex): R = R₁ + R₂ and Celsius and kelvin removed (32 equations; °C to K stays as a unit conversion); no Tick all; every equation starts ticked; Start, Clear ticks and Leave out TA moved to the top. New (12 Nov 2026): its own questions, not copied from the workouts. Tick equations (33, by unit, TA tagged) and what to include: Rearranging, Unit conversions, Multipliers, Significant figures, Show the equation. Each question picks a ticked equation, a context, the numbers and the unknown; working is givens || equation, substitution, rearranging, answer (then the unit asked for, then the s.f.). With Significant figures on, the answer must be the right value with exactly that many s.f. Hints, Show answer, and a right-first-time count per equation. Check the equation list against the spec, the contexts and value ranges, and the wording of a few questions with every option on. |

| Graphs Workout `graphs.html` | 🔍 To review | **22 Nov 2026 (later still, Alex): exam wording and a ruler grip.** The tangent questions now ask what the exam asks: v–t “By drawing a tangent to the curve, determine the acceleration of the car at a time of 20 s” (4PH1/1P May 2024 Q12(a)) in the stem, then (a) Draw your tangent, (b) find it from the gradient; cooling curves use either that single-question style (4PH1/2PR Jan 2022 Q7(a), rate of cooling) or the split style “(i) On the graph, draw a tangent to the curve when the temperature is 40 °C, (ii) Calculate the gradient of the tangent to find the rate of temperature change” (4PH1/2PR May 2025 Q7(c); either sign accepted). The line can be grabbed anywhere along it, or by a grip in its middle, and slid like a ruler. **22 Nov 2026 (later, Alex): students draw the tangent.** A blue line they move anywhere (drag an end to turn it, drag the line or its grip to slide it; keyboard: arrows move an end, Space swaps ends, Shift + arrows slide it). It starts flat and low, away from the point. Marked: it must touch the curve at the point (within 0.8 of a small square) and match its gradient (10%); if it misses the point, the point is then marked. The cooling-curve tangent starts with “find the time at X °C” only when Read a Value is ticked as well; with just Tangent ticked it starts at the tangent. Check: dragging the line on a laptop and a phone. **22 Nov 2026 (TODO 3j, third release): 13 new kinds, 37 in all, and four new types of graph** (the chooser’s left column now has 11; Unit 4 is a new topic on the right). **Tangent to a Curve** (Harder): a curved v–t graph (car, scooter, drone, skydiver speeding up; speedboat, bicycle slowing down) or a cooling curve; the student draws the tangent (see above), then finds the gradient of their own tangent (5%, or within 5% of the true value). **Intercept and Extrapolate** (Harder): p–T from 0 to 100 °C on an axis from −350 °C, ruler line of best fit (tighter: 1 small square), read where it meets zero pressure (own line ±10 °C), then what absolute zero is and whether p is proportional to T in °C; mass–volume: ruler, the cylinder’s mass from the intercept, the density from the gradient. **Interpret a Graph** (Easier, except heating curves): v–t and d–t graphs lettered A to E (which section slows down, is stationary, has the greatest acceleration, moves fastest; describe a section; which feature gives the distance, acceleration or speed; compare two speeds), match four small graphs P–S to a motion (d–t and v–t mixed, so a flat line is a trap), force–extension (where Hooke’s law holds and how the graph shows it; a rubber band that does not obey it), heating and cooling curves in sections (TA). **Explain the Shape** (Harder): three choices, one per stretch of a v–t graph (A, B to C), worded from the mark schemes’ chain (4PH1/1P Jan 2022 Q6(b)(iii), 4PH0/2P Jan 2017 Q6(c)): a base jumper or a steel ball in oil, a van or racing drone with a constant driving force, a skydiver opening a parachute. **New contexts:** cooling curves (read a temperature and a time, then why it cools more slowly; Unit 4), thermistor and LDR (read R, then I = V/R or V = IR with unit changes; plot and curve of best fit). Check: the tangent ruler on a laptop and a phone, the 10% tangent tolerance, the extrapolation tolerance, the Explain options (the right one is the longest), and the contexts. Checked with a Playwright run answering every kind (37 × 10 questions, right first time) and a 380px overflow check. **22 Nov 2026 (difficulty):** an Easier | Mixed | Harder switch under Start (Mixed by default), used with the type and topic ticks. Easier (13): the one-step reads, gradients and area, the straight-line plots (Hooke, resistor I–V), sketch decay and I–V, which relationship, anomaly. Harder (11): oscilloscope, refractive index, stopping distance, the other four plots, the other three sketches, x × y. Tags are `lv` in ROWS. Check: Harder with only Anomaly ticked says to change the difficulty. **22 Nov 2026 (latest): the chooser has two columns.** Left, By Type of Graph: the seven skills only (no contexts listed). Each row shows its right-first-time score (by unit from now on). Students can click an axis value to draw their own reading line, and on gradient questions click two points to draw a triangle (no numbers). Transverse or Longitudinal; Spring Slack, Medium or Tight (0.5, 1.0, 1.5 m/s); sliders for the frequency of the hand and the amplitude; + ¼ period steps the wave on; the tag P (and Q at IAL and IB) is dragged along the spring. IAL and IB add coil Q, the distance P to Q and the phase difference, and a Pressure graph button for the longitudinal wave. **Polarisation (IAL only, Part switch, `?show=pol`):** a lamp or a laser, one or two polarising filters, a light sensor, with the oscillations seen along the beam drawn under it and the reading against angle. **Experiment** (`?show=exp`): the wavelength at five frequencies, measured across several whole waves; IGCSE plots λ against f and shows f × λ is constant, IAL and IB plot λ against 1/f for the speed. In Polarisation it plots the reading every 10°. **Questions** (`?show=predict`): IGCSE: how the hand or coil P moves, read the wavelength and type the speed or frequency, draw the new wave by dragging its first peak. IAL and IB: which way P is moving, the speed from the two graphs, the centre of a compression or rarefaction (IAL also greatest or least pressure) on a displacement graph with the spring hidden, mark where crest C is a quarter or half a period later, draw the new wave. Polarisation: choose the sketch of reading against angle. | `solar-system-models.html` | Year 9 (8L) | 🔍 To review | 16 Nov 2026 (later): the right-hand picture is now what we really see from the Earth: looking south from the UK over rooftops, Mars as a bright orange point among the real stars of Gemini, Cancer and Leo (with Pollux, Regulus, Procyon, Sirius and the Beehive cluster), the loop at its true shape (no stretching), a dot every few nights and a numbered dot each month. Check it reads clearly for Year 9. Later: Mars is brighter, with a ring, and Mars's real path (the true ellipses, so Kepler's) lies under every model's track as a faint dashed band; Ptolemy's and Copernicus's circles miss it by up to about 12° (exaggerated: the real Copernicus missed by minutes of arc), and the note says by how much; the old "circles say here" ring is gone. A fourth Model choice, No epicycles (first in the row): everything goes round the Earth on plain circles, so Mars never goes backwards and leaves the real path; the note says that is why Ptolemy added epicycles. Ptolemy's "small circle" is now called an epicycle everywhere (kept at Ptolemy's real size, about two-thirds of the big circle). New (16 Nov 2026, space batch B, 8La): Changing Models of the Solar System. **Explore:** a Model switch (Ptolemy, about 150 AD; Copernicus, 1543; Kepler, 1609) with the scientist's name, dates and one line about them, and a Watch switch. *Mars's backward loop:* the model seen from above (all six planets to Saturn and the Moon; Ptolemy with the Earth at the centre and Mars on its small circle; Copernicus with circles; Kepler with the real ellipses, a dotted circle to compare and a Stretch the ovals button) beside Mars seen from Earth against the stars over 10 months, with a dot each month and the backward part in yellow; the line of sight from the Earth through Mars; in Kepler's model a dashed ring in the sky shows where circles would put Mars. *Jupiter's moons:* the four large moons seen from above beside Galileo's telescope view, one row a night for 8 nights, and whether the evidence fits the chosen model. **Questions** (`?show=predict`, 8 a round): whose model is this (click a scientist card; Galileo is a distractor), which drawing is X's model (three diagrams, Kepler's ovals drawn stretched), which model or models a piece of evidence supports (6 pieces: Jupiter's moons, the backward loop, Tycho's measurements, faster near the Sun, not feeling the Earth move, the daily motion of the sky), and why Mars seems to go backwards in a model. Check: the dates (Kepler 1609 for the ellipses, Galileo 1610), whether the evidence answers match your lessons (for example that the backward loop supports all three models), the stretched up-and-down scale of the sky, and the wording against the 8La textbook. Decisions: `claude/8l-batch-b-2026-11-16.md`. **24 Nov 2026, teacher page:** opened on its own (not in a workout) it is for the teacher on a projector: no Questions (link to the student version), Notes button, big picture with the choices in one strip under it |
